The Spectrum of Units of Algebraic K K -theory
Shachar Carmeli, Kiran Luecke

Abstract
It is well known that the [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] and [ 0 , 2 ] [0,2] Postnikov truncations of the units of the topological K K -theories 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 1 ​ KO \mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO} and 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 1 ​ KU \mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU} , respectively, are split, and that the splitting is provided by the ( Z / 2 \mathbb{Z}/2 -graded) line bundles. In this paper we give a similar splitting for the [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] -truncation of the units of algebraic K K -theory, considered as a sheaf on affine schemes. A crucial step is to produce the splitting for 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 1 ​ K ​ ( Z ) \mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}) . Along the way we also give a complete calculation of the connective spectrum of strict units of K ⁡ ( Z ) K(\mathbb{Z}) and K ⁡ ( F ℓ ) K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}) for a prime ℓ \ell . Finally, we show that the units of algebraic K K -theory do not split as a presheaf. In fact we show they do not even split pointwise.



1
Introduction and outline

1.1
Background and motivation

For a commutative ring spectrum
R
, one can associate a
spectrum of units

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
, which plays a role analogous to that of the group of units of a classical commutative ring. The homotopy groups of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
and
R
are closely related—we have

\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\simeq\pi_{0}R^{\times}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{i}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\simeq\pi_{i}R\text{ for }i>0.

However, the nature of the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
, which consist of not only the homotopy groups but also the
k
-invariants gluing them together, is still a far from understood in general.

Algebraic
K
-theory is a vast source of commutative ring spectra. For every classical commutative ring
S
there is an associated commutative ring spectrum
K(S)
, the
K
-theory spectrum of
S
. It is thus natural to consider the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
.

For real and complex
topological

K
-theory, the spectra of units are well-understood (cf. eg.
[
2
]
). For a spectrum
X
denote by
X[a,b]
(resp.
X[a,\infty)
) the
b
-th truncation of the
a
-th connective cover of
X
(resp. the
a
-th connective cover of
X
).
Then the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU}
decomposes as the direct sum

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU}\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU}[0,2]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU}[3,\infty),

and there is a
p
-completed isomorphism
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KU}[3,\infty)\simeq\mathrm{KU}[3,\infty)
.
Similarly, for the real topological
K
-theory spectrum
\mathrm{KO}
there is a splitting

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}[2,\infty),

and a
p
-complete isomorphism

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}[2,\infty)\simeq\mathrm{KO}[2,\infty).

Thus, knowing the additive structure of these ring spectra and their spectra of units
in a small range of degrees
gives all the information needed to determine their spectra of units as a whole, at least after completion at a prime.

1.2
Main results

The primary goal of this paper is to demonstrate that a similar phenomenon occurs also in the context of algebraic
K
-theory. Namely, we show that this is the case for the Zariski sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
that assigns to each commutative ring
S
the connective spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
(cf.
Section

7
).

Theorem A

(
7.2.4
)
.

The Zariski sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
is split at 1. That is, in the category of Zariski sheaves of connective spectra on affine schemes there is an isomorphism

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[2,\infty).

Because Postnikov truncation of sheaves involves pointwise truncation
and
sheafification, this
does not
imply that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
splits in a similar way for every ring
S
. In fact, we show that such a splitting pointwise splitting does not exist.

Theorem B

(
8.0.4
)
.

The sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
does
not
split pointwise. More precisely, for the ring
S=\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2}+y^{2}-1)
we have

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)\not{\simeq}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[2,\infty).

We prove
A
by reducing the problem to the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
, in which case we also obtain information about the two summands.

Theorem C

(
4.3.3
)
.

We have

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[2,\infty),

where the first summand is isomorphic to the fiber of the map

\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2

and after completion at a prime
p
there is an isomorphism of spectra

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty).

Using the splitting of the sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
, we obtain similar splittings for individual rings
S
, under some assumptions on their low
K
-groups. For a commutative ring
S
, let
\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)
be the Picard group of the category of discrete
S
-modules.

Theorem D

(
7.2.5
)
.

Let
S
be a ring for which
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{0}(S)
and
S^{\times}\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{1}(S)
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[2,\infty).

Closely related to the spectrum of units
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is the connective spectrum of
strict units

\mathbb{G}_{m}R:=\hom_{\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}}}(\mathbb{Z},\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R)
. In fact, we
use
knowledge of the strict units to establish splittings of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
in certain cases. We give a complete calculation of
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(S)
for
S=\mathbb{Z}
or a prime field
\mathbb{F}_{\ell}
.

Theorem E

(
6.1.1
,
6.2.1
)
.

There are isomorphisms

\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{Z}/2),

\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\simeq\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}.

1.3
Methods

Our method is to reduce the splitting of the Zariski sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
to the splitting of its global sections
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
. For this reduction we use the canonical map
\mathrm{pic}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
that sends an invertible module spectrum over
S
to its
K
-theory class. To illustrate our method in a simpler case than
\mathbb{Z}
, we simultaneously treat the prime fields

\mathbb{F}_{\ell}
.

Then, we reduce questions about
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
to questions about their
T(1)
-localizations (at an implicit prime
p
). The main tool in this reduction is the Lichtenbaum-Quillen theorem for these rings, stating that their
p
-completed
K
-theory spectra diverge from the connective covers of their
T(1)
-localizations only in small degrees; for the rings in question the difference can be analyzed explicitly using knowledge of their low degree
K
-groups.

Once we are in the
T(1)
-local setting, we have new tools to study units and strict units. Namely, the strict units are governed by the power operation
\theta
from
[
9
]
, the spectrum of units
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is related to
R
itself by the Rezk logarithm map
\log\colon\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\to R

[
11
]
which is an isomorphism in large degrees, and these two operations are related by nontrivial formulas.
We use these tools to access the homotopy type of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
and construct the desired splittings of their Postnikov towers.

One difficulty in passing from
R:=K(S)
to
L_{T(1)}R
is that the Postnikov tower of the latter
does not split
. The reason is that
\pi_{0}L_{T(1)}R
, which in essentially all examples considered here is
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, has too many units. For this reason, we work with a variant of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
which manually omits the extra units formed by
p
-completion or
T(1)
-localization (cf.
2.0.1
).

1.4
Acknowledgements

We would like to thank John Rognes for many helpful conversations, especially for explaining the proof of
2.2.1
.
S.C. would like to thank the Azrieli Foundation for their support through an Early Career Faculty Fellowship.

2
Preliminaries for the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R

In this section we discuss some general reduction steps towards splitting the Postnikov tower of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
. As mentioned above, it will be convenient to work with the following variant of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
.

Definition 2.0.1
.

For any commutative ring spectrum
E
define
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}E
to be the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{gl}_{1}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}E}

Note that for the rings
S
under consideration,
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(S)\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
, so we
alternatively wish to split the first layer of the Postnikov tower of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(S)
.
From now on we shall consider splittings of the Postnikov tower of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
for various commutative ring spectra
R
.
More precisely, we consider the following property for
X=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
.

Definition 2.0.2
.

We say that a connective spectrum
X
is
split at
m
if there is an isomorphism
X\simeq X[0,m]\oplus X[m+1,\infty)
.

Thus, our main result can be now reformulated as saying that for
R=K(\mathbb{Z})
or
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
, the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
is split at
1
.

2.1
p
-completion

In this section, we related the splittings of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
to those of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p}
for various primes
p
.
First, we discuss the relationship between splittings of a spectrum
X
and its
p
-completions in general.

Lemma 2.1.1
.

For a natural number
m
,
let
X
be a spectrum concentrated in degrees
[0,m]
and
Y
be a spectrum concentrated in degrees
[m+2,\infty)
. Then, the map

[X,Y]\rightarrow\prod_{p}[X,Y_{p}]

is injective.

Proof.

Let
F
be the cofiber of the map
Y\to\prod_{p}Y_{p}
.
By the long exact sequence associated with the fiber sequence

\mathrm{Map}(X,F)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)\rightarrow\prod_{p}\mathrm{Map}(X,Y_{p})

it would suffice to show that
\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}(X,F)=0
.
By the arithmetic fracture square of
Y
, we see that
F
is a rational spectrum, and since both
Y
and
\prod_{p}Y_{p}
are concentrated in degrees
[m+2,\infty)
,
F
is concentrated in degrees
[m+1,\infty)
.
Since there are no non-trivial maps between rational spectra concentrated in disjoint homotopical degrees, we deduce that

[X,F]=0

and the result follows.
∎

By a simple instance of obstruction theory, this allows us to relate the splittability of
X
and of its
p
-completions.

Corollary 2.1.2
.

Let
X
be a connective spectrum with finitely generated homotopy groups. Then
X
splits at
m
if and only if
X_{p}
splits at
m
for all primes
p
.

Proof.

First, if
X
splits then
X\simeq X[0,m]\oplus X[m+1,\infty)
. Taking
p
completion commutes with the truncations of
X
since
X
has finitely generated homotopy. Hence, by
p
-completing the splitting above we obtain a splitting

X_{p}\simeq X_{p}[0,m]\oplus X_{p}[m+1,\infty)

as desired.

Conversely, note that

X
splits if and only if the edge map
\alpha\colon X[0,m]\to\Sigma X[m+1,\infty)
of the cofiber sequence

X[m+1,\infty)\rightarrow X\rightarrow X[0,m]

is null homotopic. By
2.1.1
, this is equivalent to the vanishing of each of the compositions

X[0,m]\xrightarrow{\alpha}\Sigma X[m+1,\infty)\rightarrow\Sigma X[m+1,\infty)_{p}\simeq\Sigma X_{p}[m+1,\infty).

The above composition fits as the diagonal dashed arrow in the commutative square

\textstyle{X[0,m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Sigma X[m+1,\infty)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{p}[0,m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{p}}
\textstyle{\Sigma X_{p}[m+1,\infty)}

in which the vertical maps are induced from the
p
-completion
X\to X_{p}
. By our assumption that
X_{p}
is split at
m
, the bottom horizontal map labeled
\alpha_{p}
is
0
, and hence also the diagonal dashed arrow, yielding the result.
∎

We turn to discuss splittings of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
for a commutative ring spectrum
R
.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring spectrum with finitely generated homotopy groups. Then, for every prime
p
the
p
-completion map
R\to R_{p}
induces an isomorphism

(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R)_{p}\simeq(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p})_{p}.

Proof.

Note that since
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
has finitely generated homotopy groups,

\pi_{i}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\to\pi_{i}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R)_{p}
is a (classical)
p
-completion. In fact, since
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p}
has finitely generated
\pi_{0}
and
p
-complete connected cover, the same hold for it.
It would therefore suffices to show that the map
\pi_{i}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\to\pi_{i}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p})
induces an isomorphism on (classical)
p
-completions. For
i=0
this map is already an isomorphism by definition (both groups are
\{\pm 1\}
), and for
i>0
this map can be identified with the map
\pi_{i}R\to\pi_{i}R_{p}
, which is a
p
-completion since
R
has finitely generated homotopy groups.
∎

We are ready to reduce the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
to that of the spectra
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p}
.

Corollary 2.1.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring spectrum with finitely genetated homotopy groups and let
m\geq 1
. If
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p}
splits at
m
for every prime
p
, the
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
splits at
m
.

Proof.

By the “if” direction of
2.1.2
, it would suffice to show that
(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R)_{p}
splits at
m
for every prime
p
. By
2.1.3
, this spectrum is isomorphic to
(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p})_{p}
. By the ”only if’ direction of
2.1.2
, this follows from the assumption that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R_{p}
splits at
m
.
∎

2.2
T(1)
-localization

In this section we use Lichtenbaum-Quillen type results to reduce the calculation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)_{p}
to that of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}L_{T(1)}K(S)
for the rings
S
under consideration.
As usual, we denote by
L_{T(1)}X
the localization of the spectrum
X
at the Bousfield class of
T(1)=\mathbb{S}/p^{k}[v_{1}^{-1}]
and leave the prime
p
implicit
1
1

1

Note that this is the same as the
K(1)
-localization, and we use the
T(1)
-localization mostly for notation reasons.
. The canonical map
X\to L_{T(1)}X
factors through the
p
-completion; in fact
L_{T(1)}X\to L_{T(1)}X_{p}
is an equivalence. The localization functor is symmetric monoidal, so if
R
is a commutative ring spectrum, the localization map is one of commutative ring spectra.
We shall need the following fact, which is a special case of the Lichtenbaum-Quillen conjecture.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

(
[
16
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
6
]
)
For
S=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
or
\mathbb{F}_{\ell}
with
\ell\neq p
, the map

K(S)_{p}\rightarrow L_{T(1)}K(S)

induces an isomorphism

K(S)_{p}\simeq L_{T(1)}K(S)[0,\infty)

Proof.

For the case of
K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
and
p\neq\ell
this essentially follows from Quillen’s calculation in
[
10
]
, but see Theorem 5 of
[
6
]
and the discussion preceding it.

Now we turn to
K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])
. We learned this argument from John Rognes. Dwyer and Mitchell (
[
8
]
1.4, cf. also
[
7
]
8.8) show, using the work of Thomason
[
14
]
, that the Lichtenbaum-Quillen conjecture implies that
K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])\rightarrow L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])
is an equivalence on 1-connected covers. Since Voevodsky proved the Lichtenbaum-Quillen conjecture in
[
16
]
, it remains to consider
\pi_{0}
. This follows from a calculation in etale cohomology (cf.
[
12
]
Section 2).

Note that for
p=2
the desired result can also be deduced from the fiber square

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

∎

From this, we can relate the spectrum of units of
K(S)
and its
T(1)
-localization.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

For
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
or
R=K(\mathbb{Z})
, we have the following:

1.

For a prime
\ell\neq p
:

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}).

2.

There is a cofiber sequence

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z}).

Proof.

For
(1)
, this follows from
2.2.1
and the fact that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
depends only on the connective cover of a commutative ring spectrum.

For
(2)
, again by
2.2.1
we can identify the map

K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\rightarrow L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with the map

K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\rightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}.

Taking
p
-completion from the localization sequence

K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])\rightarrow\Sigma K(\mathbb{F}_{p})

and using that by Quillen’s computation
K(\mathbb{F}_{p})_{p}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
we get the map

K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\to K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}

induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{i}
for
i\neq 1
and an injection on
\pi_{1}
with cokernel
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Hence, the same holds for the map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}
and the result follows.
∎

2.3
The homotopy type of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]

We shall now determine the homotopy type of the
1
-truncation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
for the (
p
-complete) ring spectra
R
under consideration. This calculation will be useful later in the construction of sections for the map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R[0,1]
.

Remark 2.3.1
.

Recall that a connective, 1-truncated spectrum
X
is determined by the data of
\pi_{0}X,\pi_{1}X
and the multiplication by the Hopf element
\eta\in\pi_{1}\mathbb{S}
, i.e. the map

\eta\cdot\colon\pi_{0}X/2\rightarrow\pi_{1}X

More precisely, one recovers
X
from this data as the fiber of the composition

\pi_{0}X\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}X/2\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma^{2}\pi_{0}X/2\xrightarrow{\eta}\Sigma^{2}\pi_{1}X.

In particular,
X
splits as a sum
X\simeq\pi_{0}X\oplus\Sigma\pi_{1}X
if and only if
\eta
acts by
0
on
\pi_{0}X
.

Returning to the determination of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
, first we note that the case of an odd prime
p
is easy.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
R
be a
p
-local commutative ring spectrum for an odd prime
p
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\pi_{1}R.

Proof.

By definition
\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\simeq\pi_{1}R
. Since
\pi_{1}R
is
p
-local and hence has no
2
-torsion, the map
\eta\colon\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\to\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
is the zero map and we obtain the desired splitting by
2.3.1
.
∎

We turn to the more interesting case of
p=2
.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

For a prime
\ell\neq 2
, the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2})[0,1]\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})[0,1]
is the fiber of the map

\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{Sq^{2}}\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{1\mapsto-1}\Sigma^{2}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})^{\times}_{2}.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\simeq\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}
and
\pi_{0}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, we see that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2}
has the correct homotopy groups. By
2.3.1
, it remains to show that the map

\eta\colon\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2})\to\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2})

sends the non-trivial element of the group
\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
to the element
-1\in\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2}\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}
.

Now, there is a canonical map of spectra

\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2}

which is given on
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
by the reduction mod
2
and
2
-completion maps respectively:

\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}.

Hence, it would suffice to show that the map
\eta\colon\pi_{0}\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\to\pi_{1}\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
sends the generator
\Sigma\mathbb{F}_{\ell}\in\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
to the element
-1\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\simeq\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}
. This follows from the facts that on the Picard spectrum of a ring
S
we have

\eta\cdot L=\dim(L)

(see, e.g.,
[
5
, Proposition 3.20]
) and that
\dim(\Sigma S)=-1\in S^{\times}
.

∎

We turn to the case
R=K(\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 2.3.4
.

The spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{2}[0,1]
is the fiber of
Sq^{2}:\mathbb{Z}/2\rightarrow\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, by
2.3.1
it suffices to show that the map
\eta\colon\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})\to\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})
is the non-zero map. Here, as in the case of
\mathbb{F}_{\ell}
, one can use the map
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, this map is given on
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
by the maps
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
respectively, so it is enough to show that
\eta
takes the generator of
\pi_{0}\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})
to a non-zero class. This follows as above from the fact that

\eta\cdot\Sigma\mathbb{Z}=-1\in\pi_{1}\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z}).

∎

Passing from the
2
-completion to the
T(1)
-localization introduces an extra summand to
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}
.

Lemma 2.3.5
.

At the prime
p=2
, we have

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\simeq\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]

Proof.

Recall that
L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])_{2}
.
It follows from
2.2.2
that we have a cofiber sequence

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Since the map
\pi_{1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\pi_{1}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can be identified with the
2
-completion of the
2
-adic valuation map
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}
it is in particular a surjection. Hence, the above sequence induces a cofiber sequence

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}

bewtween the
1
-truncations.
The resulting boundary map
\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\Sigma\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]
is trivial on
\pi_{1}
and hence lifts to a map of spectra

\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma^{2}\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2.

Since
\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}_{\text{Sp}}(\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2)=0
the boundary map must vanish and the sequence splits.

∎

2.4
Splitting
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R

One can further simplifty
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
by removing its
\pi_{0}
completely. The resulting spectrum is
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R:=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R[1,\infty)
. Splitting the bottom homotopy group from
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
is strictly easier in general than splitting
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
from
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
. Indeed, a splitting

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty)

induces, by taking connected covers, a splitting

\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R\simeq\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty).

While the converse does not hold in general, splitting
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
is an important step towards splitting
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
, so we show the existence of such splittings for our ring spectra of interest first.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
R
be a ring such that the truncation map
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R[0,1]\simeq\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is split. Then
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is a subgroup of
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
via a map
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R=[\Sigma\mathbb{Z},\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R]\rightarrow[\Sigma\mathbb{Z},\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R].

Proof.

This is immediate. The desired map is induced by any choice of splitting
\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R=\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
S
be a ring for which
\pi_{1}K(S)\simeq S^{\times}
(e.g., a local ring). Then, there is a splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)
as

\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)\simeq\Sigma(S^{\times})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)[2,\infty).

If, moreover,
K(S)
has finitely generated homotopy groups, we get a corresponding factorization

\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)_{p}\simeq\Sigma(S^{\times})_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)_{p}[2,\infty).

Proof.

The result after
p
-completion follows immediately from the first claim by taking
p
-completions from the decomposition.
The map
\mathrm{pic}(S)\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
restricts to a map

\Sigma S^{\times}\simeq\mathrm{pic}(S)[1,\infty)\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[1,\infty)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}_{1}K(S)

which is a section of the
1
-truncation map and hence induces the desired splitting.
∎

3
Strict units and the logarithmic fiber

3.1
Preliminaries

The main advantages of a
T(1)
-local ring spectrum in studying its units are the logarithm map and the power operation
\theta
.
The first is a morphism of spectra

\log\colon\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\rightarrow R,

defined by Rezk using the Bousfield-Kuhn functor (
[
11
]
).

Definition 3.1.1
.

For a
T(1)
-local ring spectrum
R
, we let
\mathrm{F}_{\log}{R}
be the fiber of the map
\log\colon\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\to R
.

Closely related to the logarithm is the power operation
\theta
, which defines a map
\theta\colon\Omega^{\infty}R\to\Omega^{\infty}R
. When restricted to the units, it precisely cuts out the space of strict units via the fiber sequence (of spaces!)

\Omega^{\infty}\mathbb{G}_{m}(R)\rightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\xrightarrow{\theta}\Omega^{\infty}R.

The main calculations of this section are based on a comparison between the long exact sequences associated to
\log
and
\theta
. We will a few key lemmas.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let
R
be a
T(1)
-local ring. Then

\hom(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{F}_{\log}R)\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}R.

Proof.

Since
R
is
T(1)
-local, we have
\hom(\mathbb{Z},R)=0
. Hence,
the result follows by applying
\hom(\mathbb{Z},-)
to the fiber sequence

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\xrightarrow{\log}R.

∎

Lemma 3.1.3
.

Let
R
be a
T(1)
-local ring. Then

\mathrm{F}_{\log}(R)
is 2-truncated.

Proof.

For an
L_{1}
-local commutative ring spectrum
R
, let
\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}}{R}
be the fiber of the
L_{1}
-localization map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\to L_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
. By
[
1
, Theorem 4.11]
,
\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}}(R)
is
1
-truncated. Consider the commutative diagram where the rows are fiber sequences

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The right vertical arrow is the top row of the chromatic fracture square, so its fiber agrees with that of the bottom row of the chromatic fracture square which is a rational spectrum. Call this fiber
Q
. Note also that the fiber of the map
\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}}R\to\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
agrees with
\Omega Q
.

Since
\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}}R
is 1-truncated, the homotopy groups of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
in degrees
\geq 3
agree with those of
\Omega Q
, which are rational. But on the other hand, the homotopy groups of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
sit in an long exact sequnce with the homotopy groups of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
and
R
, which in degrees
\geq 3
are the homotopy groups of a
T(1)
-local ring, and hence derived
p
-complete. Hence, these groups vanish and
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is 2-truncated.
∎

Remark 3.1.4
.

It is possible for
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
to have nontrivial
\pi_{2}
.
Indeed, exactly that happens in the case of
R=KU_{p}
.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

For any
T(1)
-local ring
R
,
\mathbb{G}_{m}R
is
2
-truncated and
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
is torsion free.

Proof.

First,
\Omega\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq\hom(\mathbb{Z},\Omega\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R)
is
p
-complete since
\Omega\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is. Let
\mu_{p}(R):=\hom(\mathbb{Z}/p,\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R)
. Hence, by the long exact sequence of homotopy groups associated with the fiber sequence

\mu_{p}R\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}R\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{G}_{m}R,

the claim is equivalent to the fact that
\mu_{p}(R)
is
1
-truncated, which follows from the vanishing of the
T(1)
-local suspension spectrum of
B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/p
(see, e.g.,
[
5
, Proposition 4.1]
).
We can also argue using the logarithmic fiber: the truncatedness claim follows immediately from
3.1.2
and
3.1.3
.
Consider the fracture square for
L_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
.

Let
Q
be the fiber of bottom row. Since there are no maps from
\mathbb{Z}
to a
T(1)
-local spectrum, we find that
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{Z},L_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R)=\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{Z},Q)=Q
and is therefore rational.
As mentioned before, the fiber
\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}}
of

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\rightarrow L_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
1-truncated. Therefore the fiber sequence

\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{F}_{\text{ahr}})\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}R\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{Z},L_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R)

shows that
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
must inject into a rational group, so it is torsion free.
∎

Lemma 3.1.6
.

Let
R
be a
T(1)
-local ring. Suppose that
\theta_{1}\colon\pi_{1}R\to\pi_{1}R
(at the basepoint
1\in R
) is zero. Then the map
\log_{1}\colon\pi_{1}R\to\pi_{1}R
is also zero.

Proof.

The general formula for the relationship between log and
\theta
in degree 0 (i.e. between
\log_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
) is Theorem 1.9 of
[
11
]
. In loc. sit. just below, there is a simplified formula: if
\epsilon^{2}=0
then
\log_{0}(1+\epsilon)=\epsilon-\theta_{0}(\epsilon)
.

Let
x
be an element in
\pi_{1}R
. Write
\alpha
for the square zero suspension class in
R^{1}(S^{1})
. Note that
R^{*}(S^{1})
is a free
\pi_{*}R
-module on
1
and
\alpha
. For
z=z_{1}1+z_{\alpha}\alpha\in R^{*}(S^{1})
write
\langle\alpha,z\rangle:=z_{\alpha}
and
\langle 1,z\rangle:=z_{1}
for the coefficients in
\pi_{*}R
. Then

\theta_{1}(x)=\langle 1,\theta_{0}(1+x\alpha)\rangle,

\log_{1}(x)=\langle 1,\log_{0}(1+x\alpha)\rangle.

Since
\alpha^{2}=0
we may apply Rezk’s simplified logarithm formula to deduce that

\log_{1}(x)=\langle 1,\log_{0}(1+x\alpha)\rangle=\langle 1,x\alpha-\theta_{0}(x\alpha)\rangle.

On the other hand
\theta_{0}
is a
\delta
-structure
2
2

2

It satisfies the equations required to make
x\mapsto x^{p}+p\theta_{0}(x)
a ring map.
(
[
11
]
1.8) so that
\theta_{0}(1+\epsilon)=\theta_{0}(\epsilon)-\epsilon
whenever
\epsilon^{2}=0
. Hence

\langle 1,x\alpha-\theta_{0}(x\alpha)\rangle=\langle 1,-\theta_{0}(1+x\alpha)\rangle.

Stringing these together gives

\log_{1}(x)=-\theta_{1}(x).

∎

We see immediately that
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
is a subgroup of
(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}
. But from the splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
(cf.
2.4.2
),
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
contains
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
as a subgroup and must therefore be equal to it. That forces
\theta_{1}
to be zero. Note that
4
4

4

In degree 0,
\theta
is the unique delta structure on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, whose kernel (among units) are the units
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
the kernel (i.e. preimage of
0
) of the function
\theta_{0}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
. We record the consequences as a lemma for reference purposes.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
. For all
p\neq\ell
,
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}
and there is a split short exact sequence

0\rightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

The statement about
\pi_{1}
and the short exact sequence have been established in the discussion leading up to the lemma. It is split since for odd
p
the torsion in the base is invertible in the fiber and for
p=2
the base is trivial.
∎

Now we turn to the logarithmic fiber, again with
R:=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
. Since
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is
2
-truncated and
\hom(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{F}_{\log}R)\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}R
(
3.1.2
), the top non-vanishing homotopy group of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
agrees with that of
\mathbb{G}_{m}R
. Hence,

\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\simeq\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}.

Now consider the long exact sequence‘ associated with the log fiber sequence:

We have deduced that
\theta_{1}=0
above
3.3.1
, so by
3.1.6
we find that
\log_{1}=0
as well. Now
\log_{0}
is the pre-composition of the
p
-adic logarithm with the
x^{p-1}
map, so its kernel is tor
(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})
. Again, we collect the consequences in a lemma.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

For all
p\neq\ell
and
R:=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
, we have that
\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0
,
\pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}
, and there is a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\rightarrow\text{tor}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})\rightarrow 0.

When
p
is odd the sequence is split.

The fact that it also splits at
p=2\neq\ell
will follow from the integral case by functoriality (cf.
3.4.5
).

3.4
Strict units in
L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})

We turn to calculate the strict units and logarithmic fibers of the
T(1)
-local
K
-theory of
\mathbb{Z}
.

3.4.1
The case
p>2

The key facts about
R:=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
are

1.

The identification
L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}
.

2.

The corresponding splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
(cf.
Section

2.4
),

3.

\pi_{0}R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
\pi_{1}R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and
\pi_{2}R=0
, and
\pi_{3}R
is torsion (in fact it’s zero for
p>3
)(cf.
[
3
]
, bottom of page 7 and top half of page 10. See also
[
4
]
2012.07951 middle of page 4).

The
\theta
LES becomes

Since
\pi_{3}R
is torsion, so is
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
. By
3.1.5
we deduce that
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=0
. From the splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
(cf.
2.4.2
) we find that
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
contains
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as a subgroup, and must therefore be equal to it. It follows that
\theta_{1}=0
. Since again the kernel of
\theta_{0}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, we deduce that

\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}.

As usual we collect these results in a lemma.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
p>2
. Then
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
.

Now we turn to the logarithmic fiber. Again,
3.1.2
immediately tells us that
\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0
and
\pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
Now consider the log LES (cf.
3.1.3
)

We have deduced that
\theta_{1}=0
above, so by
3.1.6
we find that
\log_{1}=0
as well. So
\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is an extension of the kernel,
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, of the degree zero logarithm by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. So we immediately get the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
p>2
. Then

\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0,\ \pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}\ \text{and}\ \pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}.

p=2

5

As usual, this forces
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}R
to be torsion, and hecne zero by
3.1.5
. So
\theta_{2}
is an isomorphism, as it is an injection of finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-modules. From the splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}R
(cf.
2.4.2
) we find that
\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}
contains
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
as a subgroup, and must therefore be equal to it. We deduce that
\theta_{1}=0
and

\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2

(note that at
p=2
,
\theta_{0}
is injective). As usual we collect some facts in a lemma for reference.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
R:=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
p=2
. Then

\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2.

Now we turn to the logarithmic fiber
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
. Again,
3.1.2
immediately implies that
\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0
and
\pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Again,
\theta_{1}=0
and
3.1.6
force
\log_{1}=0
, and so
\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is an extension of
\mathbb{Z}/2
(the kernel of the degree 0 logarithm) by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. We deduce the following:

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Let
R:=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
p=2
. Then

\pi_{2}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=0,\ \pi_{1}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2,\ \text{and}\ \pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times A,

where
A
is either
\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\mathbb{Z}/2^{2},
or
\mathbb{Z}/4
.

Remark 3.4.5
.

We will eventually resolve the extension problem and show that
A\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2^{2}
(cf.
4.2.4
).

4
The connective homotopy type of the logarithmic fiber

The main results of this section are calculations of the connective cover of the logarithmic fibers:
4.1.1
,
4.1.4
,
4.2.1
,
4.2.5
, as well as splittings of the integral units of
L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
:
4.2.2
,
4.2.6
, and the splitting of the integral units of
K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}
:
4.3.3
.

We summarize them in the following table for the reader’s convenience. Write

\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}:=\mathrm{Fib}(\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{Sq^{2}}\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{1\mapsto-1}\Sigma^{2}(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})^{\times}_{2}),

\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2:=\mathrm{Fib}(\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{Sq^{2}}\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2).

Ring
R

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]

K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}),\quad p
odd

\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\oplus(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}

\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}

K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}),\quad p=2

(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]

\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}

K(\mathbb{Z}),\quad p>2

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\oplus\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}

\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}

K(\mathbb{Z}),\quad p=2

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}

\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}

4.1
For
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}

When
p\neq\ell
we have already calculated the homotopy groups of the logarithmic fiber (
3.3.2
). For odd
p
we can immediately describe the connective homotopy type since there is no possibility of a
k
-invariant.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}
with
\ell\neq p>2
. Then the connective cover of the logarithmic fiber is

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\oplus\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}.

Proof.

The homotopy groups of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)
are computed in
3.3.2
. Since this spectrum is
1
-truncated and
p
-local for an odd prime
p
, it splits to a direct sum according to its homotopy groups.
∎

We also immediately get a splitting of the integral units, and this time we can include the case
\ell=p
.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}
with
p>2
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty).

Proof.

By
2.3.2
we have
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}
. Hence, it remains to show that the
k
-invariant

\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\to\Sigma\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty)

is trivial. The
\Sigma(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}
-component of this map vanishes by the splitting of
\mathfrak{sl}_{1}
(
Section

2.4
), and the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-component vanishes since
\Sigma\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty)
is
p
-local and hence receives no maps from
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

∎

When
p=2
we had an unresolved extension problem in
\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2
(where here again the symbol
\rtimes
stands for an unspecified extension of the two groups). Note that because
\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}R=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}
is half the size of
\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
(cf.
3.3.1
), there must be a
k
-invariant connecting
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
. In other words, multiplication by
\eta
must be non-trivial on the homotopy of this spectrum. But we can say more.

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2}
with
\ell>2
. Then
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
splits at
1
if and only if the surjection of groups
\pi_{0}\mathrm{F}_{\log}R=(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2
is split.

Proof.

First, the map
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
factors through
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
as is evident from the log LES.
Then, consider the following diagram, in which the upper vertical maps are the inclusions of the connected covers:

Since the composition of the top row in an equivalence and the columns are fiber sequences, the outer square of the bottom rectangle is a pullback square. Hence, a section of the bottom horizontal composition provides a section of the map

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
, which then can be composed with the map
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
to provide the desired section of the
1
-truncation map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
.

Conversely, suppose the truncation map is split. Then since the induces inclusion of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
into
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
is null after
T(1)
-localization, it must factors through
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
by its universal property as a fiber. This gives a section
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\to\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)
which gives on
\pi_{0}
the desired splitting of the bottom row.

∎

By functoriality of the log LES for ring maps, the
\pi_{0}
-splitting hypothesis in
4.1.3
is implied by the
\pi_{0}
-splitting hypothesis of
4.2.3
, which is proved in
4.2.4
. So assuming these later results for the moment we get the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{2}
with
\ell\neq p=2
. Then connective cover of the logarithmic fiber is

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1].

Note that the homotopy type of the second summand is determined in Section
Section

2.3
.We a also get a statement about the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
, this time with
p
and
\ell
unrestricted

Lemma 4.1.5
.

Let
R=K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty).

Proof.

We have just proved it for
\ell\neq 2=p
. We have proved it for
p>2
in
4.1.2
. When
\ell=2=p
,
R=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and the result is trivially true.
∎

4.2
For
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})

We have already calculated the connective homotopy groups of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
. As in the case of
K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
, when
p>2
there is no possibility of a
k
-invariant.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with
p>2
. Then the connective cover of the logarithmic fiber
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\simeq\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{p}\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Similarly
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
is automatically split.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with
p>2
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty).

At
p=2
we have an extension problem to solve (cf.
3.4.5
).

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with
p=2
. The truncation map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
is split if and only if the group map
A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2
is split.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
4.1.3
. The map
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}R
factors through
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
and we arrive at the following diagram

in which the columns are fiber sequences. The upper composition is an equivalence, so the bottom rectangle is a pullback square. Hence, a section of the composition on the bottom row provides a section of
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
, and hence of the truncation map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
.

Conversely, since there are no non-zero maps from
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]
to
R
, a section of the map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
gives in particular a map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1]\to\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)
which provides a section of the bottom composition. Such a section
s\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times A
must have trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-component and hence land in
A
.

∎

Recall that the group
A
is one of
\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/4
, or
\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. We will show now that the last option is the one that occures, and in particular that the map to
\mathbb{Z}/2
from
4.2.3
indeed splits.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

A\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Proof.

R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The right vertical map is an isomorphism, and the left vertical map is the projection onto
\mathbb{Z}/2
, so the middle map is a isomorphism
A\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

∎

Combining
4.2.3
and
4.2.4
we get the following two results.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with
p=2
. Then the connective cover of the logarithmic fiber
\mathrm{F}_{\log}R
is

\mathrm{F}_{\log}R[0,\infty)\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[0,1].

From this, we deduce the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
:

Corollary 4.2.6
.

Let
R=L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
with
p=2
. Then
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R
splits at
1
, so that we have (cf. Table
4
)

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\ltimes_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}}\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}R[2,\infty).

Proof.

This follows from the combination of
4.2.3
and
4.2.4
.
∎

4.3
For
R=K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}

Lemma 4.3.1
.

For all
p
, the fiber sequence (cf.
2.2.2
)

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{Z})_{p})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}).

is split (ie. the first map is null).

Proof.

By
4.2.2
and
4.2.6

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}L_{T(1)}K(\mathbb{Z})
contains
\Sigma\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as a summand. The second map of the above fiber sequence induces an equivalence onto the complementary summand.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

Note that there is also a fiber sequence

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\rightarrow K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])

but first map is not null.

This immediately implies the desired splitting for
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}
.

Corollary 4.3.3
.

For every prime
p
the truncation map

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[0,1]

is split. When
p>2
,
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[0,1]\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
and when
p=2
,
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{2}[0,1]\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]
.

5
The homotopy type of the units of
K(S)

5.1
Splitting the bottom piece of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)

Theorem 5.1.1
.

The spectra
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
are both split at
1
. Namely, the truncation maps

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})[0,1]

admit right inverses.

Proof.

Note that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}K(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover the homotopy groups of
K(\mathbb{Z})
are finitely generated. Having split the truncations
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{Z})_{p})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}^{\pm 1}(K(\mathbb{Z})_{p})[0,1]
(cf.
4.3.3
), we can now apply
2.1.4
.

The splitting for
K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
is proved in exactly the same way, using the
p
-complete splittings from
4.1.5
.
∎

5.2
The homotopy type of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)

We can use the splittings in
5.1.1
to describe the entire homotopy type of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
by identifying the complimentary summands
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[2,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})[2,\infty)
, at least after completion at a prime
p
.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

For every prime
p
there are equivalences

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty),

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p}[2,\infty).

Proof.

From the first display of
Section

4.3
we know that

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}[2,\infty).

Since
K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}
is the connective cover of its
T(1)
-localization (cf.
2.2.1
) we have a logarithmic identification

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}[2,\infty).

On the other hand, the second fiber sequence in
Section

4.3
(in
4.3.2
) implies that
K(\mathbb{Z}[1/p])_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty)
. So we get equivalences

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty)\simeq K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}[2,\infty).

The argument for
K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
is analogous.

∎

5.3
Relation to strict triviality of
\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}

Consider the element of
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})
represented by the
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}
. This admits the structure of a strict element of
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})
, as represented by the dashed arrow in the diagram below, where the middle column is a fiber sequence. In fact this strict structure is canonical, since it corresponds to a choice of nullhomotopy of the composite of multiplication by 2 and quotienting by 2. This strict element of
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})
produces an element
[\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}]\in\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})
is the composite of the dashed and horizontal arrow in the following diagram

Note that the horizontal map is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
and surjective on
\pi_{0}
. Therefore, if the aforementioned element of
\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})
were zero, the map
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
would factor through the cofiber of the dashed arrow and produce a section of the Postnikov truncation
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]
, providing an alternative proof of
5.1.1
.
Note, however, that the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
does not by itself implies the strict triviality of
[\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}]
: it is not clear that the map
\mathrm{pic}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
factors through the summand
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
. We leave this point for future investigation.

6
The spectrum of strict units

6.1
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})

From the calculation of the logarithmic fibers (cf.
4.2.1
and
4.2.5
) we find that
\pi_{\geq 2}\mathbb{G}_{m}(K(\mathbb{Z})_{p})=0
. Consider the arithmetic fracture square

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{G}_{m}(\prod_{p}K(\mathbb{Z})_{p}\otimes\mathbb{Q}),}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Assembling various information above (eg.
3.4.1
,
3.4.3
, and
Section

4.3
) in the associated long exact sequence, we find

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\times\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\text{tor}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{\times},}

where
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\prod_{p}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is the ring of finite Adeles.
Because
[\mathbb{Z},X]=\pi_{0}X
if
X
is rational, the final map factors through the forgetful map from
\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}
to
\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
, and is therefore the conglomerate of maps
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\text{tor}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
that factor through projection to the second factor for odd
p
and the zero map for
p=2
. Since
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module we have a complete calculation of the spectrum of strict units.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

We have

\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus\Sigma(\mathbb{Z}/2).

6.2
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})

From the calculation of the logarithmic fibers (cf.
4.1.1
and
4.1.4
) we find that
\pi_{\geq 2}\mathbb{G}_{m}(K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})_{p})=0
. Assembling various information above (eg.
3.3.1
) via the (LES associated to the) arithmetic fracture square, we find

Because
[\mathbb{Z},X]=\pi_{0}X
if
X
is rational, the final map factors through the forgetful map from
\pi_{0}\mathbb{G}_{m}
to
\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
, and is therefore the conglomerate of maps
(\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times})_{p}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
that factor through projection to the second factor (and then the map induced by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
. Since
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module we have a complete calculation of the spectrum of strict units.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{F}_{\ell})=\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}\oplus\Sigma\mathbb{F}_{\ell}^{\times}.

7
The splitting of the sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K

7.1
Sheaves of Spectra

Let
\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq(\calg{Z}^{\heartsuit})^{\mathrm{op}}
be the site of affine schemes
6
6

6

For set theoretic reasons, one should restrict to affine schemes which are the Zariski spectra of
\kappa
-compact rings for suitable cardinal
\kappa
. Since the
K
-theory functor is accessible, this issue will not cause trouble and we keep it implicit.
, endowed with the Zariski topology.
Let
\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
be the
\infty
-category of presheaves of connective spectra on
\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, and let
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp})
be the full-subcategory of Zariski sheaves. For
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
and a ring
S
, we shall denote
\mathscr{F}(S):=\mathscr{F}(\Spec(S))
. Note that
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
is a symmetric monoidal localization of
\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp})
via the sheafification functor

L_{\text{Zar}}\colon\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}}),

and hence inherits a canonical symmetric monoidal structure.

Recall that the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp})
has a
t
-structure in which the positive part is
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
. Accordingly, for a sheaf
\mathscr{F}
we can set

\mathscr{F}[a,b]:=\tau_{\geq a}\tau_{\leq b}\mathscr{F}
, with respect to this
t
-structure. Similarly, we can form the sheaves
\pi_{i}\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\heartsuit})
, which we regard as discrete objects of
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
.

Remark 7.1.1
.

All the above constructions can be defined explicitly as the sheafifications of the corresponding (pointwise) operations on presheaves. For example,
\mathscr{F}[a,b]
is the sheafification of the presheaf given by
A\mapsto\mathscr{F}(A)[a,b]
, e.t.c.

We can thus define the analog of
2.0.2
in the context of sheaves.

Definition 7.1.2
.

We say that a sheaf of connective spectra
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})

splits at
a
if
\mathscr{F}\simeq\mathscr{F}[0,a]\oplus\mathscr{F}[a+1,\infty)
.

Warning 7.1.3
.

We warn the reader right away that since sheafification is involved in the formation of Postnikov truncations of sheaves, the fact that a sheaf
\mathscr{F}
splits at
a

does not imply
that
\mathscr{F}(S)
splits at
a
for every ring
S
. In fact, we will produce a concrete counterexample of this phenomenon in
8.0.4
.

7.2
K
-theory sheaf

The goal of this section is to show that the spectrum of units of
K
-theory, considered as a Zariski sheaf, splits at
1
. We begin by introducing this sheaf.
Since algebraic
K
-theory satisfies Zariski descent
[
15
]
, we have a commutative algebra object
K\in\calg(\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}}))
(where we consider here the connective
K
-theory for convenience).
Taking units produces a sheaf of connective spectra

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\in\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})

by the formula

(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K)(S):=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}(K(S)).

Remark 7.2.1
.

Note that since
K
-theory preserves filtered colimits, the stalks of the
K
-theory sheaf at the points of
\Spec(S)
are the
K
-theory spectra of the localizations of
S
. Hence, the same holds for
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
.

For a connective spectrum
X
, let
\underline{X}
denotes the constant Zariski sheaf on
X
, that is, the sheafification of the constant presheaf with value
X
. Then for each
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
, there is a natural identification

\mathrm{Map}(\underline{X},\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{F}(\mathbb{Z})),

exhibiting the functor
X\to\underline{X}
as left adjoint to the evaluation-at-
\mathbb{Z}
functor
\mathrm{PShv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})\to\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}}
.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

The lowest homotopy sheaves of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
are given by

\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\simeq\underline{\mathbb{Z}/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m},

where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
here stands for the sheaf of units on
\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We start with
\pi_{1}
.
Let
\mathrm{pic}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
be the Picard sheaf, taking
S
to invertible objects of
\Mod_{S}(\text{Sp})
. The maps
\mathrm{pic}(S)\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
are natural in
S
and hence assemble to a morphism of sheaves
\mathrm{pic}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
. Taking loops, we get a map
\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq\Omega\mathrm{pic}\to\Omega\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
. We claim that this map induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
-sheaves. Indeed, this can be checked at all the stalks, and since both functors preserve filtered colimits, it thus suffices to check this at local rings. The claim then follows from the fact that
K_{1}(S)\simeq S^{\times}
when
S
is a local ring.

For
\pi_{0}
, we have a canonical map

\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{0}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}))\to(\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K)(\mathbb{Z})

which corresponds to a map
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/2}\to\pi_{0}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
. To see that this map is an isomorphism, we may again restrict to local rings, where the claim follows from the fact that
\pi_{0}K(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
when
S
is a local ring.

∎

Lemma 7.2.3
.

There is a pushout square in
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}};\text{Sp}^{\mathrm{cn}})
of the form

\textstyle{\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1].}

Proof.

First, the existence of the commutative square follows immediately from the universal property of the constant sheaf construction. In other words, there is a natural commutative square of functors in
S
of the form

\textstyle{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]}

which sheafifies to the square in the lemma.

To show that our square is a pushout, it is enough to show that the map between the vertical cofibers is an isomorphism. Since the vertical maps are the connected covers of the
1
-truncated sheaves in the bottom row, this is equivalent to the fact the bottom horizontal map induces an isomorphism
on
\pi_{0}
, which in turn follows from
7.2.2
.

∎

We can now produce the splitting of sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
.

Theorem 7.2.4
.

The sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
splits at
1
. Namely, there is an isomorphism
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[2,\infty)
of sheaves of connective spectra on
\mathrm{Aff}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We will construct a section
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
. By
7.2.3
, this amounts to producing a commutative square of the form

\textstyle{\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K.}

with the same left vertical and upper horizontal maps as in
7.2.3
. In other words, we have to construct maps

\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K

together with a homotopy between the resulting pair of maps
\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
.

•

To construct the right vertical map, we proceed as in
2.4.2
. Namely,
consider the map
\mathrm{pic}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
; taking loops, we get a map
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\simeq\pi_{1}\mathrm{pic}\to\Omega\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
whose adjoint is a map
\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
as desired.

•

To construct the bottom horizontal map, by the adjunction between the constant sheaf functor and evaluation-at-
\mathbb{Z}
, it suffices to construct a map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
. Such a map is provided by the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})
in
5.1.1
.

•

To show that the resulting maps from
\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})}
to
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
are homotopic, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}(\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})},\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}),\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2,

where the first isomorphism is by the constant sheaf adjunction, the second by the fact that
K_{1}(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, the third by the adjunction between
\hom(\mathbb{Z},-)
and the forgetful functor from
\mathbb{Z}
-modules to spectra, and the last by the computation of
\mathbb{G}_{m}K(\mathbb{Z})
in
6.1.1
.
Unwinding these identifications, we see that a map
\alpha\colon\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
is determined by the composition

\Sigma\mathbb{S}\to\Sigma\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq(\underline{\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})})(\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\alpha(\mathbb{Z})}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z}).

It is now easy to check that for the constructions above, these composites both give the non-trivial class in
\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, and hence they are homotopic.

Finally, to get the splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
from the map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
, we have to check that it induces isomorphisms on the
\pi_{0}
- and
\pi_{1}
-sheaves. Note that in the square of
7.2.3
, the left vertical map is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
-sheaves and the lower horizontal map is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
-sheaves. Hence, the result follows from the facts that the maps
\underline{\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(\mathbb{Z})[0,1]}\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
and
\Sigma\pi_{1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
in the square from the beginning of the proof also induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
respectively, which is clear by
7.2.2
.
∎

We mention again that the result above does not imply that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
splits at
1
for all
S
(we disprove this in the following section). Instead, the spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
contains the sections of the
sheaf

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]
over
\Spec(S)
as a summand. On the positive side, in the cases where the sections of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1]
coincide with the
1
-truncation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
, we do get a splitting for
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
at
1
.
We finish by spelling out this condition a bit more explicitly and give a few examples.

For a commutative ring
S
let
\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)
be the Picard group of the abelian category of discrete
S
-modules.
Recall that there is a map
K_{0}(S)\to\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)\oplus\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(S)

taking a virtual projective
S
module to its determinant line bundle and its (locally constant) rank function, respectively.
Similarly, in degree
1
there is a determinant map

K_{1}(S)\to S^{\times}
.

Proposition 7.2.5
.

Let
S
be a commutative ring for which the maps
K_{0}(S)\to\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)\oplus\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(S)
and
K_{1}(S)\to S^{\times}
are isomorphisms. Then
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
splits at
1
.

Proof.

By
7.2.4
it would suffice to show that the canonical map
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]\to(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K[0,1])(S)
is an isomorphism. This map is obtain from the map of commutative ring spectra
K(S)[0,1]\to K[0,1](S)
by applying the functor
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}(-)
, so it suffices to show that the ring map is an isomorphism.

Now, we have a fiber sequence

\Sigma\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow K[0,1]\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}

and the sheaf
\Sigma\mathbb{G}_{m}
has homotopy groups

\pi_{0}((\Sigma\mathbb{G}_{m})(S))\simeq\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{1}(\Sigma\mathbb{G}_{m})(S)\simeq S^{\times}.

Thus, we obtain a canonical isomorphism
\pi_{1}(K[0,1](S))\simeq S^{\times}
and an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(K[0,1](S))\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(S).

This sequence is in fact split by the map
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(S)\to K(S)\to K[0,1](S)
that picks the trivial modules of given (locally constant) rank, so that
\pi_{0}(K[0,1](S))\simeq\underline{\mathbb{Z}}(S)\oplus\mathrm{Pic}^{\heartsuit}(S)
.
It is straightforward to check that via these isomorphisms the maps
\pi_{i}K(S)\to\pi_{i}K[0,1](S)
for
i=0,1
correspond precisely to the maps from the statement of the proposition, so the result follows from our assumptions on
S
.
∎

There are many rings satisfying the conditions.

Example 7.2.6
.

Every local ring, as well as every Euclidean domain, satisfies the conditions of
7.2.5
(see, e.g., ). Hence, for these rings we obtain natural splittings
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)\simeq\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[2,\infty)
.

8
A counterexample to point-wise splitting of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K

Observe that
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
does not split as a Zariski
presheaf
—indeed, this would imply that the presheaf
S\mapsto\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]
is a summand of the sheaf
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K
, and hence a sheaf itself, which it is clearly not. One may still wonder whether
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
splits for every ring
S
(and just not functorially so). In this section we show that even this is false: we produce a ring
S
for which
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)\to\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[0,1]
is not split. The motivating idea is that, in view of
7.2.5
counterexamples should come from
SK_{1}(S):=\ker(\det:K_{1}(S)\to S^{\times})
. A simple example of a ring with non-trivial
SK_{1}
is

S:=\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2}+y^{2}-1),

which is the example we shall consider for the rest of this section.

Definition 8.0.1
.

Let
\text{tr}:K(\mathbb{R}[i])\to K(\mathbb{R})
be the map induced by restriction of modules along
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}[i]
. Write
\text{Cofib}(\text{tr})
for the associated cofiber.

Lemma 8.0.2

(cf.
[
13
]
)
.

There is a cofiber sequence

K(\mathbb{R}[i])\xrightarrow{(0,\text{tr})}K(\mathbb{R})\oplus K(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow K(S).

In particular,
K(S)\simeq K(\mathbb{R})\oplus\text{Cofib}(\text{tr})
.

Proof.

The scheme
\Spec(S)
is isomorphic to the complement of the pair of complex conjugate points
\{[1:i],[1:-i]\}
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}{R}
(which we view as a point with residue field
\mathbb{C}
). The cofiber sequence is the localization sequence for the open-closed decomposition

\mathbb{P}^{1}{R}=\{[1:i],[1:-i]\}\cup\Spec(S)
.
∎

Lemma 8.0.3

(cf. e.g.
[
17
, Example 1.5.4]
)
.

There is an isomorphism

\pi_{1}K(S)\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2},

generated by the image of
\eta
under the unit map
\mathbb{S}\to K(S)
and the element of
GL(S)/E(S)
represented by the following element of
SL_{2}(S)
:

\begin{bmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Here,
E(S)\subseteq\mathrm{GL}(S)
is the normal subgroup generated by elementary matrices.

Proof.

Consider the long exact sequence calculating the homotopy groups of
\text{Cofib}(\text{tr})
:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

On
\pi_{0}
the transfer is multiplication by 2. On
\pi_{1}
the transfer is given by the norm map
(x+iy)\mapsto x^{2}+y^{2}
. Together with the facts that
\pi_{2}K(\mathbb{R}[i])=0
,
\pi_{1}K(\mathbb{R}[i])=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and
\pi_{1}K(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{\times}
this shows that
\pi_{1}\text{Cofib}(\text{tr})\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
. Together with
8.0.2
this shows that
\pi_{1}K(S)\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
, as well as the claim that
\eta
can be taken to be one generator. To finish the proof, note that the
2\times 2
matrix in question represents a nontrivial class in
\mathrm{GL}(S)/E(S)
(where
E(S)
is the normal subgroup generated by elementary matrices), and it has determinant 1. Therefore it must represent the generator of
SK_{1}(S)\simeq\pi_{1}\text{Cofib}(\text{tr})
.

∎

Theorem 8.0.4
.

The connective spectrum
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
does not split at
1
.

Proof.

Note that the
\mathbb{R}
-points of
\Spec(S)
are topologically equivalent to
S^{1}
. Hence there is a ring map
K(S)\rightarrow KO(S^{1})
. Moreover, observe that this map induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
. Indeed,
KO(S^{1})\simeq KO\oplus\Sigma^{-1}KO
is generated by the two maps
S^{1}\rightarrow O
given by the constant map at
-1\in O
, and the inclusion of groups
S^{1}=SO(2)\rightarrow O(2)\rightarrow O
. The former is detected by
\eta
under the composite
K(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow K(S)\rightarrow KO(S^{1})
. The latter is detected by the other generator of
\pi_{1}K(S)
in the presentation of
8.0.3
, as is evident from the matrix presentation provided there.

Now suppose
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)
were split at 1. Then

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[1,\infty)\simeq\Sigma(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}K(S)[2,\infty).

As the comparison map to
\mathrm{KO}(S^{1})
is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
, this would force a splitting

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}(S^{1})[1,\infty)\simeq\Sigma(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}(S^{1})[2,\infty).

However,
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}(S^{1})
is the connective cover of
(\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO})(S^{1})=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}\oplus\Sigma^{-1}\mathfrak{gl}_{1}KO
. By considering the second summand, we see that a splitting as in the last display would force a splitting

\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}[2,\infty)\simeq\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathfrak{gl}_{1}\mathrm{KO}[3,\infty).

However, such a splitting does not exist. Indeed, such a splitting would immediately imply that
\pi_{2}\mathbb{G}_{m}\mathrm{KO}_{2}
contains
\mathbb{Z}/2
as a summand, which violates
3.1.5
.

∎
